) yield some complication of the heat kernel evaluation as
mentioned in the Introduction.

To start with, let us choose suitable charts on

as follows. For

It is possible to adapt the seemingly unusable formulas in

Remark 6.2

(6.9)

||K(\cdot,\cdot)||_{C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)}:=\sum_{j}\sup_{w,\eta}||k^{j}((\cdot,w),(\cdot,\eta))||_{C^{s}(V_{j}\times V_{j})}

which means the supremum over all
x,

y
in the domain
,
of all
partial derivatives of
k^{j}
in
z
,
\zeta

\in

V_{j}
up to order
s.
Note that the
C_{B}^{s}
-norm (
6.9
) depends on the choice of the
expression (
6.8
) with “
m",
but we do not put on
such dependence whenever no confusion occurs.

Lemma 6.3
.

With the notation above,
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}P_{m,t}^{0}(u)=\pi_{m}u
(pointwise) for every
u

\in

\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)

\cap

L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).
Moreover, for every integer
s\geq 0
it holds that
P_{m,t}^{0}(u)

\rightarrow

\pi_{m}u
as
t\rightarrow 0
in the norm
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
(hence in
L^{2}
) for
u\in\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).
In
particular
P_{m,t}^{0}(u)

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma).

Remark 6.4
.

For
u_{t},

u_{0}

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
assume
u_{t}

\rightarrow

u_{0}
in
C_{B}^{0}.
Then it is
easy to see that
u_{t}

\rightarrow

u_{0}
pointwise on
\Sigma
and
in
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma)
(in view of Remark
3.4
). In particular, if
u

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
with
||u||_{C_{B}^{0}}

=

0
then
u=0.

Remark 6.5
.

The presence of the projection in Lemma
6.3
(rather
than the identity operator in the usual case) reflects the transversal
feature of
P_{m,t}^{0}.

Proof.

(of Lemma
6.3
)
For
u

\in

L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}),

\pi_{m}u

\in

L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m})
and by (
6.4
)
\pi_{m}u|_{W_{j}}

=

\eta^{m}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)
for some
\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)

\in

L^{2,\pm}(V_{j}),

we compute

(6.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}(H_{m,t}^{j}(\pi_{m}u))(x)\overset{(\ref{6-1.5})+(\ref{6-1})}{=}\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\int_{\Sigma}\{\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\eta^{-m}\tau_{j}(\zeta)\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}\eta^{m}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)\}dv_{\tilde{V}_{j}}(\zeta)dv_{f,m}(\eta)

\displaystyle\overset{}{=}\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\int_{V_{j}}\{\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta)\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)\}dv_{V_{j}}(\zeta)\int_{C_{\varepsilon_{j}}}l(y)^{m}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)dv_{f,m}(\eta)

By (
6.2
) and hence

(6.11)

\int_{C_{\varepsilon_{j}}}l(y)^{m}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)dv_{f,m}(\eta)=1

(cf. (
3.26
) with
\tau_{p_{0}}^{\ast}
dropped as
remarked after (
3.27
))
,
the above simplifies to

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0+}\int_{V_{j}}\{\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta)\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)\}dv_{V_{j}}(\zeta),

then, since
K_{t}^{j}

\rightarrow

I
in the distribution
sense as
t

\rightarrow

0
(cf.
[
6
, Definition 2.15 (4) on p.75 ]
) it further simplifies to
\varphi_{j}(x)\tau_{j}(z)(\pi_{m}u)(z,w)
by (
6.4
). From this, (
6.6
),
\tau_{j}(z)

=

1
on supp
\varphi_{j}
and
\Sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}(x)=1,
the first assertion of the
lemma follows. It also follows from the expression in the last paragraph of

[
6
, p.85]
that
P_{m,t}^{0}(u)

=

\sum_{j}H_{m,t}^{j}(\pi_{m}u)\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma).

By the argument similar to (
6.10
)

\displaystyle P_{m,t}^{0}(u)(z,w)-(\pi_{m}u)(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}w^{m}\varphi_{j}(x)[\int_{V_{j}}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta)\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)dv_{V_{j}}(\zeta)-\tau_{j}(z)\upsilon_{j}(z)].

Hence for some constant
C(s)

>

0,
as
t

\rightarrow

0

||P_{m,t}^{0}(u)-(\pi_{m}u)||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq C(s)\sum_{(j,k)\in I}||K_{t}^{j}(\tau_{j}\upsilon_{j})-\tau_{j}\upsilon_{j}||_{C^{s}(V_{j})}\rightarrow 0

by directly applying
[
6
, Theorem 2.20(2)]
. This proves the
second assertion (convergence in
L^{2}
by Remark
6.4
)..

The first task is aimed to express the kernel function
(H_{m,t}^{j}\circ\pi_{m})(x,y)
(see the RHS of (
6.6
)) in a more manageable form.
This is given in Proposition
6.9
below.

To start with, suppose that
(z,w)
denotes local coordinates around
x

\in

\Sigma.
Let
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\circ x
denote the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-orbit of
x.
We define
\tau_{x}

:

\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}

\rightarrow

\eta\circ x

\in

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\circ x.

Recall the definition of
L^{2,\ast}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
(resp.
L_{m}^{2,\ast}(\Sigma,G_{a,m}),

\pi_{m})
in
Remark
5.6
. We first express
\pi_{m}(u)(x)
in the following
proposition:

Proposition 6.6
.

For the orthogonal projection (recall that
a>\frac{m}{2}\geq 0
)

\pi_{m}:L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})\equiv L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m})\rightarrow L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m})

it holds that for
u\in L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})

(6.12)

\pi_{m}(u)(x)=l(x)^{m}\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}u(\xi\circ x)(\bar{\xi})^{m}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)

where
\tau_{x}
is defined precedingly. Moreover if
u
is
smooth,
\pi_{m}(u)
remains smooth. Here
\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}:
(
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M})_{\xi\circ x}

\rightarrow
(
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M})_{x}
denotes the pullback of forms.

Remark 6.7
.

i)
Note that since
u
is
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
-valued and the action
\sigma
leaves
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}

invariant, we can trivialize
\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}u(\xi\circ x)
and
view it as coefficient function(s) with respect to a basis of global
sections (
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-equivariant) of
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}

along the orbit
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\circ x.
We then write
\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}u(\xi\circ x)
as
u(\xi\circ x)
by abuse of notation in the
proof below. See Footnote
6
(seated above (
6.13
)) for further
details.

ii)
The action
\sigma
preserves the metric
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}
endowed
on
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
. There is another metric
G_{a,m}|_{\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}}
on
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
induced from
G_{a,m}
when
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
is viewed as a subbundle of
\Lambda^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
(see Remark
5.6
). We have that
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}

=

G_{a,m}|_{\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}}
(cf. Lemma
8.10

i)
) and thus
\sigma
preserves the metric
G_{a,m}|_{\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}}
too.

Remark 6.8
.

i)

L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
is not trivial: Using a local bump function/section (cf.
Remark
6.7
)
u
with value
u(\xi\circ x)
close to
\xi^{m}
and
support near
x

=

(z,w=1)
in coordinates such that
\pi_{m}(u)(x)

>

0
as
l(x)
and the measure
(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)
are
positive, yields the desired result.

ii)
There exists a fixed
u
contained in
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a})
for any
a>0
and
a>\frac{m}{2}\geq 0
such that
\pi_{m}(u)

=

0
in
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a})
(which by Lemma
3.5
equals
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
): Let
u
be a local function/section
supported near
w=1/2
in a chart
(z,w),
depending only on
z
,
r=|w|

such that for
m=0
the integral (
6.12
) of

over

equals
0

with respect to
G_{a}
for any
a>0;
this is made possible because of (
3.18
) where the parameter “
a
” has the impact on
G_{a}
only outside a neighborhood of
\{w=1/2\}.
Since the angular
integral of
u
is seen to vanish for
m>0
(cf. (
6.12
) and (
7.24
))
,
altogether we obtain the claim.

Proof.

(of Proposition
6.6
)
Recall that

is defined in (

). Locally

=

h(z(x),\bar{z}(x))w(x)\bar{w}(x)

\in

The orthogonal projection
\pi_{m}
is characterized by the following conditions:

i)

\pi_{m}
is a bounded linear operator on

ii)

\pi_{m}(u)\in L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m})
for

\in

L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}),

iii)

\pi_{m}\circ\pi_{m}=\pi_{m}

iv)

\pi_{m}^{\ast}=\pi_{m},
i.e.,
\pi_{m}
is
self-adjoint.

v)

\pi_{m}(u)=u
for
u\in L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m}).

Now we claim that

defined by (

) satisfies the above

(see (

) for the definition of

in local coordinates

for

and

for

=

\xi w

(see Footnote
5
5

5

,

. Hence

by (

).
)
,
we write (see Footnote
6
6

6

In view of Remark
6.7

ii)
: for

Write a global section

and

). Compute

\displaystyle\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}u(\xi\circ x)

\displaystyle=

.
)

(6.13)

\displaystyle\pi_{m}(u)(x)\overset{l=hw\bar{w}}{=}h^{m}|w|^{2m}\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}u(z,\eta)(\bar{\eta})^{m}(\bar{w})^{-m}(\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\eta)

\displaystyle\overset{|w|^{2m}(\bar{w})^{-m}=w^{m}}{=}

\displaystyle w^{m}h^{m}(z,\bar{z})\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}u(z,\eta)(\bar{\eta})^{m}(\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\eta).

By Remark

To prove

\displaystyle|\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}u(z,\eta)(\bar{\eta})^{m}(\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\eta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}|u(z,\eta)|^{2}(\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\eta)\int_{\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}|\eta|^{2m}(\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\eta).

We now estimate, by (

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{W}_{j}}|\pi_{m}(u)(x)|^{2}dv_{\Sigma,m}(x)\overset{(\ref{proj3})+(\ref{volume})}{\leq}\int_{z\in\tilde{V}_{j}}{\big\{}\int_{w\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}h^{m}|w|^{2m}\tau_{(z,1)}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(w)

To show

if

is further assumed to be in
L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m});
see the Footnote
6
above for explanation of
u(\xi\circ x).
For
x

\in

\Sigma\backslash\Sigma_{\text{sing}}

\displaystyle\pi_{m}(u)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l(x)^{m}\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}\xi^{m}u(x)(\bar{\xi})^{m}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u(x)\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}l)^{m}(\xi)(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)=u(x)

where (
7.20
) and
l^{m}(\xi\circ x)

=

(\tau_{x}^{\ast}l)^{m}(\xi)
via definitions (resp. (
3.27
)) are used for the
second (resp. third) equality. Condition
v)
follows
.

For the general case let us first prove the following:

(6.22)

\displaystyle\text{For the sake of clarity let us denote by }\tilde{\pi}_{m}(u)\text{
the RHS of }(\ref{6-1e}).

\displaystyle\text{Let }u_{j}\rightarrow u\text{ in }L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,G_{a,m})\text{.
Then }\tilde{\pi}_{m}(u_{j})\rightarrow\tilde{\pi}_{m}(u)\text{ in }L^{2}\text{.}

Proof of (
6.22
):
The
L^{2}
-difference
||\tilde{\pi}_{m}(u_{j})-\tilde{\pi}_{m}(u)||_{L^{2}}^{2}
is bounded by

I_{j}:=\int_{\Sigma}l(x)^{2m}\left[\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}|u_{j}(\xi\circ x)-u(\xi\circ x)||\xi|^{m}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)\right]^{2}dv_{\Sigma,m}(x).

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the integral over
\xi

gives

(6.23)

\displaystyle I_{j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}{\big\{}l(x)^{m}\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}|u_{j}(\xi\circ x)-u(\xi\circ x)|^{2}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\cdot\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}l(x)^{m}|\xi|^{2m}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi){\big\}}dv_{\Sigma,m}(x).

Observe that
l(x)^{m}|\xi|^{2m}

=

l(\xi\circ x)^{m}
by (
7.20
) and hence
\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}l(x)^{m}|\xi|^{2m}(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)

=

1
by (
3.27
). It is
possible to simplify (
6.23
) further: Substituting this into (
6.23
) and making a change of variables
y=\xi\circ x
over
\Sigma\backslash\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
(to ensure bijectivity), we can write the
RHS of (
6.23
) as (
\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
being of measure
0
)

(6.24)

\int_{y\in\Sigma\backslash\Sigma_{\text{sing}}}\{|u_{j}(y)-u(y)|^{2}\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}l(\xi^{-1}\circ y)^{m}(\tau_{y}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi^{-1})\}dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

because
(\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)dv_{\Sigma,m}(x)
in (
6.23
) equals (
\tilde{\sigma}^{\ast}[(\tau_{y}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi^{-1})dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)]
)
(\xi,x)
(where
\tilde{\sigma}:(\xi,x)

\rightarrow

(\xi^{-1},y

=

\xi\circ x))
as proved in
Lemma
7.6

iii).
Again
\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}l(\xi^{-1}\circ y)^{m}(\tau_{y}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi^{-1})

=

1
for any
y

by (
3.27
) reduces (
6.24
) to
\int_{\Sigma}|u_{j}(y)-u(y)|^{2}dv_{\Sigma,m}
which tends to zero as
j\rightarrow\infty
by assumption
.
We have shown (
6.22
).

We can now finish the proof for the general
\tilde{u}
. Let
\bar{\chi}_{j\text{ }}
be cut-off functions with support in

(cf.

)
,
which are
\equiv

(

\supset

and
\bar{\chi}_{j}

\rightarrow

1
on

via

=

M_{\text{sing}}
as already assumed
.
Write
\chi_{j}

=

\pi^{\ast}\bar{\chi}_{j}
and consider
\chi_{j}\tilde{u}.
Then one
has the following:
a)

\chi_{j}\tilde{u}

\rightarrow

\tilde{u}
in

\in

since

i.e.

\in

Using

and applying (

) with the previously

\equiv

one checks that

easily satisfies the above conditions
i)
to
v)
as the

\pi_{m}
does, giving that

=

as desired.

For the last statement of the lemma, the continuity property of
\pi_{m}

in
C_{B}^{s}
-norm (see (
6.7
)) is postponed to Lemma
6.14
.

Define
d\mu_{y,m}(\xi),
a 2-form in
\xi
for

\in

by the map

Notice that

and one sees that it is this

).

To express

Proposition 6.9
.

Let the notation
\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}\circ y}^{\ast}

be as above.

i)
The kernel function for

\equiv

ii)

),

in two

\in

Compare Lemma

and its proof for similar

Proof.

Compute

as follows:

(6.28)

\displaystyle[(H_{m,t}^{j}\circ\pi_{m})u](x)=H_{m,t}^{j}(\pi_{m}(u))(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{p\in\Sigma}H_{m,t}^{j}(x,p)\pi_{m}(u)(p)dv_{\Sigma,m}(p)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{6-1e})}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{p\in\Sigma}H_{m,t}^{j}(x,p)\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}\sigma(\xi)_{p}^{\ast}u(\xi\circ p)(\bar{\xi})^{m}(\tau_{p}^{\ast}dv_{f,m})(\xi)l(p)^{m}dv_{\Sigma,m}(p)

We now make a change of variables:

by

=

\xi\circ p
(on

)
.

After a careful examination, (

) equals (

By rearranging terms, the above becomes (by

(noting that

=

and

acts on

(6.29)

\int_{y\in\Sigma}\left[\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(H_{m,t}^{j}(x,\xi^{-1}\circ y)\circ\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}\circ y}^{\ast})(\bar{\xi})^{m}d\mu_{y,m}(\xi)\right]u(y)dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

Now (
6.26
) follows from the integrand
\left[...\right]

in (
6.29
).

The proof of (

) will be postponed to Section 7 after studying
the properties of

. See the paragraph prior to Remark

(containing (

) through (

)). The assertion

follows from the
C_{B}^{s}
-bound (
C_{B}^{0}
enough) and Remark

.

Next we want to compute
(\partial_{t}P_{m,t}^{0})u+P_{m,t}^{0}\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\pi_{m}u
(see (

) for

, (

) for

). For use in the following

we let the local operator
(which is a notation used interchangeably with

in (

))

(6.30)

\square_{z,m}^{c\pm}:=D_{z,m}^{c\mp}D_{z,m}^{c\pm}

denote the Laplacian of (the standard
m
-th)
spin^{c}
Dirac
operator

(6.31)

D_{z,m}^{c}=D_{z,m}+A_{z,m}^{c}=\bar{\partial}_{z,m}+\bar{\partial}_{z,m}^{\ast}+A_{z,m}^{c}

on

\subset

\mathbb{C}^{n-1}
(recall (

)
for

)
.

P_{m,t}^{0}
satisfies the following
adjoint type heat equation asymptotically; compare

and see Remark

for a significant difference.

Lemma 6.10
.

It holds that

(6.32)

(\partial_{t}P_{m,t}^{0})u+P_{m,t}^{0}(\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ\pi_{m})u=R_{t}u\text{ \ for }u\in\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})

where
R_{t}

:

\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
is an operator with distribution kernel
R_{t}(x,y)
(=
R(t,x,y)

\in

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Sigma\times\Sigma,

T^{\ast 0,+}\Sigma\otimes(T^{\ast 0,+}\Sigma)^{\ast}))
of the form (
6.8
) satisfying that for every
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},
there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0,

C_{s}>0
independent of
t
such that

(6.33)

||R(t,x,y)||_{C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)}\leq C_{s}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{t}}\text{ \ for }t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Proof.

Write
\pi_{m}u|_{W_{j}}(\zeta,\eta)=\eta^{m}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)
(see
(
6.4
); we drop subscripts “
j"
on the
coordinates). Recalling that
\tilde{\square}_{m,y}^{c\pm}
acts on
y
(=
(\zeta,\eta))
while
\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}
acts on
\zeta
via
Proposition
5.3
,
we compute (
P_{m,t}^{0}
is the sum of
H_{m,t}^{j}\circ\pi_{m}
over
j
(
6.5
))

(6.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}H_{m,t}^{j}(\pi_{m}u)+H_{m,t}^{j}\tilde{\square}_{m,y}^{c\pm}(\pi_{m}u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{W_{j}}\{\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}\partial_{t}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta)

\displaystyle+\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)(\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta))\tau_{j}(\zeta)\}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}\{\partial_{t}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)+K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)(\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta))\}\tau_{j}(\zeta)dv(\zeta)

in which we have used
\int_{C_{\varepsilon_{j}}}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}dv_{f,m}(\eta)

=

1
by (
6.11
). Noting
that

\int_{V_{j}}\{\partial_{t}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)+K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}\}(\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta))dv(\zeta)=0

since
K_{t}^{j}
is the Dirichlet heat kernel (
6.3
)
,
we
reduce the RHS of (
6.34
) to

(6.35)

\displaystyle\int_{V_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)S_{j}(t,x,\zeta)\bar{\eta}^{-m}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)dv(\zeta)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{6-1d})}{=}\int_{W_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)S_{j}(t,x,\zeta)\bar{\eta}^{-m}\eta^{-m}(\pi_{m}u)(y)\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

where

(6.36)

S_{j}(t,x,\zeta)(\eta^{-m}(\pi_{m}u)(y))=w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\bar{\eta}^{m}[\tau_{j}(\zeta),\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}](\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)).

Note that
\tau_{j}(\zeta)
=
1
for
\zeta
in some small
neighborhood of the
z
-part of supp
\varphi_{j}.
It follows that
\varphi_{j}(x)S_{j}(t,x,\zeta)

=

0
if
(z(x),\zeta)
is in some
small neighborhood of
(z,z)
(due to
[\tau,\square]

=

0
there
).

The idea is that the singular part of
\varphi_{j}S_{j}
originally caused
by
K_{t}^{j}
along the diagonal (
\sim\frac{1}{t^{n-1}}
) is now
dismissed.

Note that
S_{j}(t,x,\zeta)
in (
6.35
) is a differential operator
acting on
\upsilon_{j}(\zeta).
We can convert it into a kernel function
via an integration by parts as follows: by (
6.36
), the
self-adjointness of
\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}
with
\tau_{j}(\zeta)

being of compact support, and
\int_{C_{\varepsilon_{j}}}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}dv_{f,m}(\eta)

=

1

(6.37)

\displaystyle\text{LHS of }(\ref{Adj-2-0})=\int_{V_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)[\tau_{j}(\zeta),\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}](\upsilon_{j}(\zeta))dv(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}\{\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}(K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)\tau_{j}(\zeta))-(\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm}K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta))\tau_{j}(\zeta)\}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)dv(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)\hat{S}_{j}(t,x,y)\bar{\eta}^{-m}\upsilon_{j}(\zeta)dv(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{W_{j}}\varphi_{j}(x)\hat{S}_{j}(t,x,y)\bar{\eta}^{-m}\eta^{-m}(\pi_{m}u)(y)\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

where (the
[\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm},\tau_{j}(\zeta)]
below
as a first order differential operator acts on the
\zeta
-variable of
K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta))

(6.38)

\hat{S}_{j}(t,x,y):=w^{m}[\square_{\zeta,m}^{c\pm},\tau_{j}(\zeta)](K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta))\bar{\eta}^{m}.

Therefore using an analogous argument of
[
18
, (5.47)]
for (
6.37
) (it is essential that the Gaussian factor
\exp(-\frac{\tilde{d}_{M}^{2}(z,\zeta)}{4t})
encoded in

see (

), absorbs

\frac{1}{t^{n-1}}
if
(z,\zeta)
is off the diagonal
where

=

0
as mentioned above), we conclude that for
every
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},
there exist
\varepsilon>0,

independent of

\bar{\eta}^{-m}\eta^{-m}l(y)^{m}

by (

) below)

(6.39)

||\varphi_{j}(x)\hat{S}_{j}(t,x,y)h^{m}(\zeta,\bar{\zeta})\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)||_{C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)}\leq C_{s}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{t}}\text{ for }t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

From (
6.34
) and (
6.37
) it follows that

(6.40)

(\partial_{t}P_{m,t}^{0})u+P_{m,t}^{0}(\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ\pi_{m})u=\hat{R}_{t}(\pi_{m}u)

where

(6.41)

\hat{R}_{t}(x,y):=\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}(x)\hat{S}_{j}(t,x,y)\bar{\eta}^{-m}\eta^{-m}\sigma_{j}(\vartheta)l(y)^{m}.

So (
6.32
) holds for
R_{t}

:=

\hat{R}_{t}\circ\pi_{m}
in view of (
6.40
) and (an analogue of (
6.26
))

(6.42)

R(t,x,y)\text{ }(=R_{t}(x,y))=\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(\hat{R}_{t}(x,\xi^{-1}\circ y)\circ\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}\circ y}^{\ast})(\bar{\xi})^{m}d\mu_{y,m}(\xi).

Now that

(6.43)

\bar{\eta}^{-m}\eta^{-m}l(y)^{m}=h^{m}(\zeta,\bar{\zeta})

from (
6.41
) is bounded in
\zeta

\in

M=\Sigma/\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
by Lemma

iv)
, which is compact,

(from (

) and (

(using (

) for

=

|\xi|)
and

=

1
by Corollary

, we conclude (
6.33
) for

by (
6.39
) via (
6.41
), (

). For

note the

C_{B}^{s}
-norms that concern mainly the

or
\zeta
-variables (i.e. the horizontal ones). Observe that

(6.44)

\bar{\eta}^{m}(\xi^{-1}\circ y)\bar{\xi}^{m}=\chi(y)\bar{\eta}^{m}(y)

where

\displaystyle\chi(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\eta}^{m}(e^{-i\gamma}\circ y)}{\bar{\eta}^{m}(y)}|\xi|^{-m}\bar{\xi}^{m}\text{ (}\xi=|\xi|e^{i\gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{im\theta(e^{-i\gamma}\circ y)}e^{-im\gamma}e^{-im\theta(y)}\text{ (}\eta(y)=|\eta(y)|e^{-i\theta(y)}).

Since
C_{B}^{s}
-norms involve no

(see (

)), for the

-norms of

it suffices to prove,
with (

), the smoothness of

and

by using the

-domains. We discuss this smoothness issue as
follows. For the smoothness of

(in
\chi(y)
) see a similar claim on
C_{B}^{s}
-norm in (
6.27
) (as it involves
the similar expression
(\xi^{-1}\circ y)
by (

)), whose proof is
placed after (

). In this proof

actually lies in (

), whose smoothness is proved in the last

) involving the smoothness of

The
point is that the “large
"
angle action (see Case

after (

)) due to the local freeness of the

-action makes the treatment less direct and leads to the consideration of
\alpha_{k}
. Lastly, to deal with

-norms for

in

simply notice the expression (

), (

)
with

replaced by

, which can be simplified further by the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariance of

(Lemma
7.6

ii)
) . Our proof is now
completed.

Remark 6.11
.

The difference between the
S_{j}
of (
6.36
) and the
corresponding term in the same notation
S_{j}
in
[
18
, (5.46) and p.84]
arises from that between
H_{m,t}^{j}(x,y)
of (
6.5
) and
H_{j}(t,x,y)
of
[
18
, (5.38)]
. This difference is partly due to the
fact in Lemma
7.13
that holds only at the Hilbert space level, while
some similar identifications in the proof of
[
18
, Proposition 5.1, p.73]
hold at the pointwise level. That these identifications, adapted to
their own contexts, are only similar in nature leads to the different
results. Compare Remark
3.20
for different effects caused by the
metrics here and
[
18
]
. Note that the
S_{j}(t,x,w)
of
[
18
, p.84]

is actually a (first-order) differential operator, so the presentation in

[
18
, p.84]
, especially the derivation of
[
18
, (5.47)]
, need be
fixed in a similar way as the part from
S_{j}
of (
6.36
) to
\hat{S}_{j}
of (
6.38
).

If one tries to directly show that
P_{m,t}^{0}
is an approximate heat
kernel one may encounter a difficulty which we refer to
[
18
, the paragraph
after (1.81), p.36]
for a similar situation and explanation. Suffice it
to say that it becomes easier to show that its adjoint
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
is
an approximate heat kernel. To express
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
in terms of
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}
we need the following lemma that
H_{m,t}^{j}
(hence
H_{m,t}^{j\ast})
is a bounded linear operator on
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}),
defined through the kernel function
H_{m,t}^{j}(x,y)
(see (
6.5
)).

Lemma 6.12
.

The kernel function
H_{m,t}^{j}(x,y)
as in (
6.5
)
defines a bounded linear operator on
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).
In fact
H_{m,t}^{j}(x,y)
is
L^{2}
in two
variables (
x,y)

\in

\Sigma\times\Sigma
with respect to the metric
G_{a,m}

\times

G_{a,m}.
The kernel function
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}(x,y)

defined by
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}(x,y)

:=

\overline{H_{m,t}^{j}(y,x)}

represents the Hilbert space adjoint operator
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}
. (A
superscript “
t"
meant as transpose may be placed on
\overline{H_{m,t}^{j}(y,x)},
but we omit it.)

Proof.

A “local version
"
of this (with cut-off functions in (
6.5
) removed) is seen in Remark
6.2
. Now in (
6.5
) observe
that
\eta^{-m}l(y)^{m}

=

h(\zeta,\bar{\zeta})^{m}\bar{\eta}^{m}.

From this and Remark
3.4
the assertion on the
L^{2}
-condition
follows. This yields the first and third assertions (see
[
57
, Theorem 2,
p.13 ]
).

Using Lemma
6.12
we have now

(6.45)

\displaystyle P_{m,t}^{0\ast}=\sum_{j}(H_{m,t}^{j}\circ\pi_{m})^{\ast}=\sum_{j}\pi_{m}^{\ast}\circ H_{m,t}^{j\ast}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\pi_{m}\circ H_{m,t}^{j\ast}:L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})\rightarrow L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).

It is worth noting that while the action of
P_{m,t}^{0}
may not preserve
the space
\Omega_{m}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
(cf. Lemma
6.3
), the image
of
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
on
\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
is nonetheless seated in
\Omega_{m}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
because of
\pi_{m}
. By taking the adjoints
P_{m,t}^{0\ast},

R_{t}^{\ast}
of
P_{m,t}^{0},

R_{t}
respectively,
we are going to prove the following. Denote by
\Omega_{c}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)

\subset

\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
the set of those elements (smooth
sections of
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
) of compact support in
\Sigma.

Note that
\Omega_{c}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)
is dense in
L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
(see Remark
5.6
).

Theorem 6.13
.

In the preceding notation, we have

i)

(6.46)

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u=\pi_{m}u\text{ in }||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}\text{ \ for }u\in\Omega_{c}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma).

ii)

(6.47)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial P_{m,t}^{0\ast}}{\partial t}u+\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u=R_{t}^{\ast}u\text{ \ for }u\in L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})\cap\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ }(\text{not necessarily in }\Omega_{c}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)).

iii)
The distribution kernel
R^{\ast}(t,x,y)
of
R_{t}^{\ast}
is
given by
\overline{R(t,y,x)}
(cf. (
6.33
)); it satisfies a similar
estimate as
R_{t}
in Lemma
6.10
.

Although
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}P_{m,t}^{0}u=\pi_{m}u
in
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
for
u

\in

\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})
holds by Lemma
6.3
,
it is a bit surprising that the corresponding statement for
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
is not obviously true as it might appear to be at first sight, unless
u

is further restricted
.
This difference is essentially due to the
noncompactness of
\Sigma.
Compared to the compact CR case
[
18
, Theorem
5.13, p.84]
the following proof is less trivial in that certain
elements “
\alpha_{l}\in S^{1}"
related to the local
orbifold group will be introduced (and also used in some later parts of the
paper).

For the proof of Theorem
6.13
we start by proving the following
lemma. Writing
\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)

:=

\oplus_{q}\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,q}(\Sigma)
(see Definition
3.10
) we
want the boundedness of
\pi_{m}:\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)

\rightarrow

\tilde{\Omega}_{m}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
with respect to
C_{B}^{s}
-norm. In the CR case
[
18
]
these
C_{B}^{s}
-norms were
neither needed nor were they pursued because the compactness of the total
space there simplifies the picture. The technicalities here lie in the local
freeness of the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action.

Lemma 6.14
.

With the notation above,
||\pi_{m}(u)||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq C_{1}||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}
for every
u\in\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma),

s\in\mathbb{N\cup}\{0\}.

Proof.

Write
u(x)

=

\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}(x)u(x)=\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}(x)w^{m}v_{j}(x)
where
(z,w)
are local coordinates (with
j
-dependence suppressed),
v_{j}
is a
(0,q)
-form and
\varphi_{j}
is as
in item
i)
after Notation
6.1
. Substituting it into (
6.12
),
we obtain (omitting “
\circ"
in
\xi\circ x)

(6.48)

\pi_{m}(u)(x)=l(x)^{m}\sum_{j}\int_{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}(\varphi_{j}(\xi x)w(\xi x)^{m}v_{j}(\xi x))\bar{\xi}^{m}\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(\xi).

We first want to extract
w(x)^{m}
out of
w(\xi x)^{m}
in (
6.48
). Given a local chart
W_{j}

:=

V_{j}

\times

(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})

\times

\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in Notation
6.1

and suppose
x\in W_{j},
there exist at most finitely many
\alpha_{l}
’s
\in

S^{1}

(\subset

\mathbb{C}^{\ast})
dependent on
x
such that
\alpha_{l}x

\in

W_{j}
with
\phi(\alpha_{l}x)

=

0
(recall
w

=

re^{i\phi})
because if
\alpha_{l}
is such an element then any
\alpha_{l}^{\prime}

\in

S^{1}
near
\alpha_{l}
will give
\phi(\alpha_{l}^{\prime}x)

\neq

0
by small angle action (see Case
i)

after (
6.82
)). Let
\alpha_{0}

=

e^{-i\phi(x)}
so that
w(\alpha_{0}x)

=

\alpha_{0}w(x)

(=

r(x)).
Denote
J_{l}

:=

\{\xi

\in

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}

:

-\varepsilon_{j}

<

\arg(\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1})

<

\varepsilon_{j}\}.
It follows that for
\xi

\in

J_{l},

-\varepsilon_{j}

<

\phi(\xi x)

=

\arg(\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1})+\phi(\alpha_{l}x)

=

\arg(\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1})

<

\varepsilon_{j}
hence that
w(\xi x)=\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1}w(\alpha_{l}x)

since
\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1}
is of small angle (Case
i)
after (
6.82
)). Using
\phi(\alpha_{l}x)

=

\phi(\alpha_{0}x)

=

0
together
with Lemma
7.6

i)
gives
w(\alpha_{l}x)

=

w(\alpha_{0}x)

=

\alpha_{0}w(x).
In sum, for
\xi

\in

J_{l}
we have

(6.49)

w(\xi x)=\xi\alpha_{l}^{-1}\alpha_{0}w(x)

extracting
w(x)
from
w(\xi x),
as mentioned earlier
.
Remark
that
\alpha_{l}^{-1}\alpha_{0}
is independent of
x
and
\{\alpha_{l}^{-1}\alpha_{0}\}_{l}
forms a group (see Proposition
8.7
), but we
need not use this fact here.

Write
z_{l}

=

z(\xi x)
for
\xi

\in

J_{l}.
Note that
z_{l}
is
independent of
\xi
in
J_{l}
(similarly implied by the small angle
condition as above)
.
For any fixed
j
in (
6.48
), by the cut-off
\varphi_{j}
we can now reduce the integral in (
6.48
) to

(6.50)

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\int_{\xi\in J_{l}}\xi^{m}\alpha_{l}^{-m}\alpha_{0}^{m}w(x)^{m}\varphi_{j}(z_{l},\phi(\xi x))\sigma(\xi)_{x}^{\ast}v_{j}(z_{l},w(\xi x))\bar{\xi}^{m}\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\alpha_{l}^{-m}\alpha_{0}^{m}w(x)^{m}\sigma(\alpha_{l})_{x}^{\ast}\int_{\xi\in J_{l}}\varphi_{j}(z_{l},\phi(\xi x))v_{j}(z_{l},w(\xi x))|\xi|^{2m}\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(\xi).

Observe that

(6.51)

l(x)^{m}\int_{\xi\in J_{l}}|\xi|^{2m}\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(\xi)\overset{(\ref{7-1-1})}{=}\int_{\xi\in J_{l}}l(\xi x)^{m}\tau_{x}^{\ast}dv_{f,m}(\xi)\overset{(\ref{3-43.5})}{=}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{\pi}.

The fact that
\alpha_{l}
smoothly depends on
x
is proved in
remarks after (
7.49
). This leads, via
u

=

\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}w^{m}v_{j}
and the definition of
C_{B}^{s}
-norm, to (noting that
|\alpha_{l}|=1,

w=|w|e^{i\phi}
and
\varphi_{j}
is bounded)

(6.52)

||\pi_{m}(u)||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq C_{0}\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{s}\sup_{w}||\varphi_{j}(\cdot,\phi)v_{j}(\cdot,w)||_{C^{k}(V_{j})}=C_{0}\cdot(\#\text{ of }l)\text{ }||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}.

Using the above lemma we continue with the proof of Theorem
6.13
.

Proof.

(of Theorem
6.13
)
Let us first prove that
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u

=

\pi_{m}(H_{m,t}^{j\ast}u)

converges in the
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
-norm as
t\rightarrow 0.
The
kernel function of
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}
reads as (see (
6.5
) with
x

=

(z,w),

w

=

|w|e^{i\phi},

y

=

(\zeta,\eta),

\eta

=

|\eta|e^{i\vartheta})

(6.53)

\displaystyle H_{m,t}^{j\ast}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{w^{-m}(x)\tau_{j}(z(x))\sigma_{j}(\phi(x))l(x)^{m}}K_{t}^{j\ast}(z(x),\zeta)\overline{\varphi_{j}(y)\eta^{m}(y)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{m}\tau_{j}(z)\sigma_{j}(\phi)h^{m}(z,\bar{z})K_{t}^{j\ast}(z,\zeta)\varphi_{j}(y)\overline{\eta^{m}(y)}.

For given
t
it is easy to verify that
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}u

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
. The convergence of
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}u
in the
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
-norm as
t\rightarrow 0

follows simply because
K_{t}^{j\ast}

=

K_{t}^{j}
involved in the
above expression of
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}
has the property that
K_{t}^{j}(u)

\rightarrow

u
in
C^{s}
-norm (with respect to
z)
uniformly in
“parameter
"\eta
since
u
is assumed to be of compact
support (see the bottom paragraph for the variable change to absorb the
singular part
\frac{1}{t^{n-1}}
(of
K_{t}^{j}
) in
[
6
, p.85]
).
This together with Lemma
6.14
yields
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u

\rightarrow
(say)
P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
in the
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
-norm as
t\rightarrow 0
. To prove that
P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u

=

\pi_{m}(u)
consider for
v\in\Omega_{c}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)

(6.54)

\displaystyle(P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u-\pi_{m}u,v)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u-P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u+P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u-\pi_{m}u,v)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(P_{m,t}^{0\ast}u-P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u,v)_{L^{2}}+(P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u-\pi_{m}u,v)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 0\text{ + }(P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u-\pi_{m}u,v)_{L^{2}}\text{ as }t\rightarrow 0.

On the other hand,

(6.55)

\displaystyle((P_{m,t}^{0\ast}-\pi_{m})u,v)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(u,(P_{m,t}^{0}-\pi_{m}^{\ast})v)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(u,(P_{m,t}^{0}-\pi_{m})v)_{L^{2}}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }t\rightarrow 0

by Lemma
6.3
. It follows from (
6.54
) and (
6.55
) that the limit
P_{m,0}^{0\ast}u=\pi_{m}u.
We have shown (
6.46
).

To show (
6.47
) we take the adjoint of (
6.32
) in Lemma
6.10
to get

\partial_{t}P_{m,t}^{0\ast}+[P_{m,t}^{0}(\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ\pi_{m})]^{\ast}=R_{t}^{\ast}\text{ on }\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m})

where (
\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}
is formally self-adjoint on
\Omega_{m}^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)

\supset

P_{m,t}^{0\ast}(\Omega^{0,\pm}(\Sigma)\cap L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma));
see Lemma
4.9
)

\displaystyle[P_{m,t}^{0}(\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ\pi_{m})]^{\ast}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{m}^{\ast}\circ(\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm})^{\ast}\circ P_{m,t}^{0\ast}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{m}\circ\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ P_{m,t}^{0\ast}=\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\circ P_{m,t}^{0\ast},

giving (
6.47
). The last claim of the theorem for
R^{\ast}(t,x,y)
follows from a similar estimate for
R(t,x,y)
of (
6.33
),
so that
R^{\ast}(t,x,y)
is in
L^{2}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)
and
represents the kernel function of the adjoint operator
R_{t}^{\ast}
(compare Lemma
6.12
).

Before solving our heat equation let us show that
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
is a
bounded linear operator on
\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
in
the
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
-norm uniformly for
t
near
0.
Recall the
notation
\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
in Definition
3.10
. For
u

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)
recall
the definition of
||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}
in (
6.7
).

Proposition 6.15
.

Given
\delta

>

0
and
s\in\mathbb{N\cup}\{0\},

there exists a constant
C_{s}
independent of
\delta
and
t
(but may
depend on
s)
such that (see (
6.7
) for
||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}
)

(6.56)

||P_{m,t}^{0\ast}(u)||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq C_{s}||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}

for
0\leq t\leq\delta
and
u\in\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)

\subset

L^{2,\ast}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).

Proof.

By (
6.6
)
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}

=

\sum_{j}\pi_{m}^{\ast}\circ H_{m,t}^{j\ast}

=

\sum_{j}\pi_{m}\circ H_{m,t}^{j\ast}.
We claim
that
H_{m,t}^{j\ast}
satisfies the following estimate: there exist
\delta_{0}

>

0
and
C_{s}^{\prime}

>

0
(independent of
\delta_{0}
and
t)
such that for
0

<

t

<

\delta_{0}

(6.57)

||H_{m,t}^{j\ast}u||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq C_{s}^{\prime}||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}.

for
u

\in

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,,\ast}(\Sigma).

Writing
x=(z,w)

(w=|w|e^{i\phi}),

y=(\zeta,\eta)
in local
coordinates and
u(y)

=

\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}(y)u(y)

=

\sum_{j}\varphi_{j}(y)\eta^{m}v_{j}(y),
we have by (
6.53
)

(6.58)

\displaystyle(H_{m,t}^{j\ast}u)(x)=\int_{\Sigma}H_{m,t}^{j\ast}(x,y)u(y)dv_{\Sigma,m}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{m}h^{m}(z,\bar{z})\sum_{j}\tau_{j}(z)\sigma_{j}(\phi)\int_{\Sigma}K_{t}^{j\ast}(z,\zeta)\varphi_{j}(y)\bar{\eta}^{m}\eta^{m}v_{j}(y)dv_{\Sigma,m}(y).

Observe that for each
t

>

0

\mathop{\mathrm{I}m}H_{m,t}^{j\ast}|_{\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)}

\subset

\tilde{\Omega}_{m,loc}^{0,\ast}(\Sigma)

(
Definition
3.10
). To estimate
(uniformly in
t
) the
C_{B}^{s}
-norm of the RHS of (
6.58
) is
reduced to estimating the usual
C^{s}
-norm in
z
and the supremum norm in

w
of

(6.59)

h^{m}(z,\bar{z})\tau_{j}(z)\sigma_{j}(\phi)\int_{\Sigma}|\eta|^{2m}K_{t}^{j\ast}(z,\zeta)\varphi_{j}(y)v_{j}(y)dv_{\Sigma,m}(y).

The following inequality follows from
[
6
, Theorem 2.20 or 2.29]
(cf. comments below (
6.53
) in the proof of Theorem
6.13
)
:
there exists
\delta_{0}

>

0
and
C_{s}^{\prime\prime}

>

0
(independent of
\delta_{0}
and
t)
such that for
0

<

t

<

\delta_{0}

(6.60)

\displaystyle||\int K_{t}^{j\ast}(z,\zeta)\overline{\varphi_{j}(\zeta,\eta)v_{j}(\zeta,\eta)}dv(\zeta)||_{C^{s}(z)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{s}^{\prime\prime}||\overline{\varphi_{j}(\cdot,\eta)v_{j}(\cdot,\eta)}||_{C^{s}(\zeta)}\leq C_{s}^{\prime\prime}\sup_{\eta}||\varphi_{j}(\cdot,\eta)v_{j}(\cdot,\eta)||_{C^{s}(\zeta)}.

Observe that
|\eta|^{2m}
in the integrand of (
6.59
) is
independent of
z
and its integral with respect to the fibre measure
dv_{f,m}(y)
is bounded. This together with (
6.60
) gives

\sup_{w}||\text{the term (\ref{Kstar1})\mbox{$|$}\mbox{$|$}}_{C^{s}(z)}\leq\text{(constant)}\cdot||u||_{C_{B}^{s}}.

Now (
6.57
) follows. This together with Lemma
6.14

implies (
6.56
).

One way to solve our heat equation, based on Theorem
6.13
and
Proposition
6.15
, resorts to the method of successive approximation
(cf.
[
6
]
,
[
18
]
). The convolution of two operators
A,

B
is
defined through its distribution kernel as usual:

(6.61)

(A\text{ }\sharp\text{ }B)_{t}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Sigma}A(t-s,x,p)B(s,p,y)dv_{\Sigma,m}(p)ds.

The method of successive approximation results in a solution to
our heat equation; see Proposition
6.17
below. But since
\Sigma
is noncompact, to have convergence requires a special class of
operators. Fortunately the operators
P_{m}^{0\ast}
and
R^{\ast}
belong
to this class (
P_{m}^{0\ast},

R^{\ast}
denote the operators with
distribution kernels
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}(x,y),

R_{t}^{\ast}(x,y)

respectively). The point is basically that although
\Sigma
is noncompact,
we have only one direction which is noncompact, and the integration along
this noncompact direction can be controlled by the choice of our metric
G_{a,m}
(see Remark
3.4
). Another ingredient to be used here is (
6.49
) in the proof of Lemma
6.14
above; see the proof of Lemma
6.16
below. These features give more complexities than the previous
work
[
18
, Proposition 5.14]
.

Lemma 6.16
.

The kernel functions associated to
R_{t}^{\ast},\cdot\cdot\cdot,
(
R^{\ast k})_{t}
(
=
(
R^{\ast}\sharp R^{\ast}

\cdot\cdot\cdot

\sharp R^{\ast})_{t},

k
copies) and
P_{m,t}^{0\ast},

(P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast})_{t},

(P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast}\sharp R^{\ast})_{t},\cdot\cdot\cdot
are of the form (
6.8
).
Moreover, given
s\in\mathbb{N},
there are
1>\delta_{0},

\delta_{1}>0

and
C_{s}

>

0
(independent of
\delta_{0},

\delta_{1}
and
t
)
such that for all
t\in(0,\delta_{0})

(6.62)

||R_{t}^{\ast}||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{\delta_{1}}{t}},\cdot\cdot\cdot,\text{ }||(R^{\ast k})_{t}||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}e^{-\frac{\delta_{1}}{t}},

(6.63)

||P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast}||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq\frac{C_{s}}{2}e^{-\frac{\delta_{1}}{t}},\cdot\cdot\cdot,\text{ }||P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast k}||_{C_{B}^{s}}\leq\frac{C_{s}}{2^{k}}e^{-\frac{\delta_{1}}{t}}.

Here
||R_{t}^{\ast}||_{C_{B}^{s}}
means
||R_{t}^{\ast}(x,y)||_{C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)}
(as given in (
6.9
)), etc.

Proof.

By (
6.12
) we easily obtain

(6.64)

We can now adapt

Proposition 6.17
.

i)
(Existence) Given
s\in\mathbb{N},
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for every fixed

\in

(0,\varepsilon)
the
kernel function
\Lambda_{t}(x,y)
given by

(6.73)

\Lambda_{t}(x,y):=P_{m,t}^{0\ast}(x,y)-(P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast})_{t}(x,y)+(P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast}\sharp R^{\ast})_{t}(x,y)-\cdot\cdot\cdot

exists, and converges in
C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)
(including its
t-derivatives up to any given order
)
.
ii)
Let (
R^{\ast k})_{t}
be as
in Lemma
6.16
,
k

\geq

0.
Suppose

\in

L^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).
Then
(P_{m}^{0\ast}\sharp R^{\ast k})_{t}u

\in

L_{m}^{2,\pm}(\Sigma,\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M},G_{a,m}).
In particular the image of
\Lambda_{t}
lies in the
m
-space. Moreover for

(6.74)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Lambda_{t}}{\partial t}u+\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}\Lambda_{t}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Lambda_{t}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{m}u\text{ in }||\cdot||_{C_{B}^{s}}

iii)
(Approximation) Given
s\in\mathbb{N},
there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
independent of
t
such that

(6.75)

||\Lambda_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)-P_{m,t}^{0\ast}(\cdot,\cdot)||_{C_{B}^{s}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)}\leq e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{t}}\text{ \ for all }t\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}).

Proof.

It follows from (
6.63
) that the sequence (
6.73
) converges in
the
C_{B}^{s}
-norm and (
6.75
) holds. By the definition of the
C_{B}^{s}
-norm associated with a fixed

(

), the function

of (

) exists on

For

we observe that the image of the convolution lies in the image of
its first operator. In our case the first operator is
P_{m,t}^{0\ast}
so
the image is in the
m
-space. To verify (
6.74
) takes slightly more
work. Let

denote the

-th term in (

). A direct

(6.76)

\frac{\partial q_{t}^{k}}{\partial t}(x,y)+\tilde{\square}_{m,x}^{c\pm}q_{t}^{k}(x,y)=(R^{\ast k})_{t}(x,y)+(R^{\ast(k+1)})_{t}(x,y)

(cf.
[
6
, (2) of Lemma 2.22]
). Noting that
\Lambda_{t}
is
the alternating sum of these
q_{t}^{k},
one interchanges the order of the
action of
\partial_{t}+\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm}
on
\Lambda_{t}
with
the summation in view of (
6.63
) and similar estimates on their
t
-derivatives. The first equation of (
6.74
) follows from telescoping
with (
6.76
) (cf.
[
6
, Theorem 2.23]
). The second equality of (
6.74
) follows from (
6.46
) (cf. (
6.56
)) and (
6.62
).

We are now back to discuss the properties of

in Proposition

. Similarly let

acting on the even/odd elements. Suppose

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c\pm})B_{t}^{\pm}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\text{ \ (differentiability in }t\text{ is assumed),}

The

Theorem 6.18
.

i)
(Uniqueness) It holds that

]

\displaystyle 0

It is well known that the (ordinary, local) heat kernel
K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)

has the asymptotic expansion (see for instance
[
18

)

(6.84)

\displaystyle K_{t}^{j}(z,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\tilde{d}_{M}^{2}(z,\zeta)}{4t}}K^{j}(t,z,\zeta)

\displaystyle K^{j}(t,z,\zeta)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle t^{-n+1}b_{n-1}(z,\zeta)+t^{-n+2}b_{n-2}(z,\zeta)+...

as

for

in

where

(cf. (

) with lines above). Note that

may depend on the choice of charts

a priori

we can

(as a Riemannian manifold) to be convex for every

(possibly after shrinking). Then it is seen that

here can be

is not

on the complex

since two distinct points in

may

Compare remarks after (

).

{}_{s}(t,\pi(x),\pi(y))

on the compact complex

(as one can show (

Recall the asymptotic expansion of the (ordinary, local) heat

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\tilde{d}_{M}^{2}(z,z^{\prime})}{4t}}K^{j}(t,z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle K^{j}(t,z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle t^{-n+1}b_{n-1}(z,z^{\prime})+t^{-n+2}b_{n-2}(z,z^{\prime})+...

as

It is not difficult to see via Theorem

(with its proof) that

is the distance function on orbifold charts, associated with

on

(via the projection

The reader is warned that

is in general not the distance function on

(unless the

Further, the coefficients

(s=n-1,

\cdot\cdot\cdot)
in (

(with

and are independent of the choice of charts

\ni

For, it is well known (
[

, Chapter 2]
) that the
coefficients depend only on the local geometry; since the local geometry we
use consists of

and the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant

(see (

), (

) and Step

orbifold
M

=

\Sigma/\sigma
(with
\pi:\Sigma

\rightarrow

thus intrinsic in nature.

For every compact set

\subset

V_{j},
there is a constant

such that

(7.10)

\frac{1}{C_{K}}|z-z^{\prime}|\leq\tilde{d}_{M}(z,z^{\prime})\leq C_{K}|z-z^{\prime}|.

Set

as just mentioned. Plugging (

where, for

This finishes the computation of (

(7.13)

We claim the existence of a constant

>

\displaystyle\text{Suppose }e^{-i\gamma}\circ x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tilde{z},\tilde{w})\in\hat{W}_{j}\text{ for some }\gamma\in[\varepsilon_{j},\frac{2\pi}{p}-\varepsilon_{j}].

\displaystyle\text{Then }|\tilde{z}-z|\text{ }

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\hat{\varepsilon}_{0}\hat{d}(x,\Sigma_{\text{sing}})\text{ }>0\text{.}

The Case

z,

\tilde{z}

(of (

To see

Remark 7.2
.

One expects that the function
\hat{d}(x,\Sigma_{\text{sing}})
is comparable to the genuine distance function

See
[

, Theorem 6.7]
in the CR context.

To proceed further, we need the following fact:

Lemma 7.3
.

It holds that
(
see (
3.3
) for
l(x))

(7.16)

\displaystyle l(e^{i\gamma}\circ x)=l(x)\text{ provided that }e^{i\gamma}\circ x\text{ (}e^{i\gamma}\in S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\ast})\text{ and }x\text{
lie in}

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ the same chart }W_{j}\text{ with coordinates }(z,w)\text{ (cf.
Notation \ref{n-6.1})}.

Proof.

Let
D_{0}

=

W_{j},

D_{1},

...

,

D_{L\text{ }},

D_{L+1}
be a
sequence of patches with coordinates
(z_{k},w_{k})
on each
D_{k},
and
x_{0},

x_{1},

...
,
x_{L},

x_{L+1}
be a sequence of points. Assume
the following:
D_{L+1}\equiv D_{0}
,
(z_{0},w_{0})

\equiv

(z_{L+1},w_{L+1})

\equiv

(z,w)
and
x_{0}:=x

\in

D_{0}
,
x_{k}

\in

D_{k}\cap D_{k-1},

1

\leq

k

\leq

L,

x_{L+1}

\in

D_{0}\cap D_{L}
such that

(7.17)

x_{k+1}=e^{i\gamma_{k}}\circ x_{k},\text{ }0\leq k\leq L.

We further assume: for every
0

\leq

k

\leq

L,
if
\theta\in[0,\gamma_{k}]
then
e^{i\theta}\circ x_{k}\in D_{k}.

This gives

(7.18)

w_{k}(e^{i\theta}\circ x_{k})=e^{i\theta}w_{k}(x_{k})

by (
2.7
). Since
l(q)=h(z(q),\bar{z}(q))w(q)\bar{w}(q)
in (
3.3
), (
3.4
) is independent of the choice of coordinates, from (
7.17
) and the fact that
h
is invariant under rotation (by
\gamma_{k})
by using (
7.18
) and (
3.4
), it follows that

(7.19)

\displaystyle l(x_{k+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(z_{k}(x_{k+1}),\bar{z}_{k}(x_{k+1}))w_{k}(x_{k+1})\bar{w}_{k}(x_{k+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(z_{k}(x_{k}),\bar{z}_{k}(x_{k}))w_{k}(x_{k})\bar{w}_{k}(x_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l(x_{k}),\text{ }0\leq k\leq L.

Clearly (
7.16
) follows from (
7.19
).

Remark 7.4
.

The above proof actually shows that (
7.16
) holds
unconditionally. As an important application, one sees that the metric
G_{a,m}
on
\Sigma
(in Section 3) is
S^{1}
-invariant via (
3.8
),
(
3.9
), (
3.7
) and Lemma
7.6

iv)
.

Corollary 7.5
.

It holds that for all
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
and for all

x\in\Sigma,

(7.20)

l^{m}(\xi^{-1}\circ x)=|\xi|^{-2m}l^{m}(x).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.3
and writing
\xi

=

|\xi|e^{i\gamma}
, we see
that (via Remark

)

l(\xi^{-1}\circ x)=l(|\xi|^{-1}\circ(e^{-i\gamma}\circ x))=|\xi|^{-2}l(e^{-i\gamma}\circ x)=|\xi|^{-2}l(x)

and hence the lemma.

In the remaining part of this section, we will omit
``\circ"
in the
notation of
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action.

Lemma 7.6
.

It holds that

Proof.

For

a^{\prime})\text{ }[(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{p}-\varepsilon_{j},(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{p}+\varepsilon_{j}]\text{ and }b^{\prime})\text{ }[(s-1)\frac{2\pi}{p}+\varepsilon_{j},s\frac{2\pi}{p}-\varepsilon_{j}],

Remark 7.10
.

To generalize the

estimate here to the

(
C_{B}^{l}
more precisely) estimate presents no serious problem. We skip the
details, and content ourselves with referring to
[
18
, Corollary 6.3]

for a closely related treatment.

One sees that the RHS of (
7.50
) (for general
p
) blows up as
t\rightarrow 0
and
x

\rightarrow

\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
at various
speeds (due to
t^{-(n-1)}
in the second term). Let
b_{s}^{\pm}(z,\zeta)

be coefficients in the asymptotic expansion of
K_{t}^{j,\pm}(z,\zeta)
(
\pm
means acting on even/odd degree of elements as usual) (cf. (
7.9
)):

(7.52)

\displaystyle K_{t}^{j,\pm}(z,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\tilde{d}_{M}^{2}(z,\zeta)}{4t}}K^{j,\pm}(t,z,\zeta)

\displaystyle K^{j,\pm}(t,z,\zeta)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle t^{-n+1}b_{n-1}^{\pm}(z,\zeta)+t^{-n+2}b_{n-2}^{\pm}(z,\zeta)+\text{ }{\tiny\cdot\cdot\cdot}.

Remark 7.11
.

Note that the notion of the above asymptotic expansion (
7.52
) (see
[
18
, (5.19) on p.76]
) is different from the one in
[
6
, p.87]
in which the meaning of
\sim
is given in such a way that it
includes the Gaussian term
e^{-\frac{\tilde{d}_{M}^{2}(z,\zeta)}{4t}};

our above meaning of
\sim,
excluding the Gaussian term, is basically
equivalent to the one in Chavel’s book
[
16
, (45) on p.154]
.

Let

.

Lemma 7.13
.

With the notation above, it holds that
\Psi_{\pm,m}

preserves

-inner product up to a constant, i.e.

(7.57)

(\Psi_{\pm,m}(s),\Psi_{\pm,m}(t))_{\Lambda_{m}^{0,\ast}}=\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{\pi}(s,t)_{\mathcal{E}^{m}}

for sections

of
\mathcal{E}^{m}.

Proof.

We observe that
h(z,\bar{z})^{-m}
in (

) and
|w|^{2m}
in (

) are related in the following fibre integration: (writing

=

(7.58)

\displaystyle\int_{(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}|w|^{2m}dv_{f,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}|w|^{2m}l(q)^{-m}d\hat{v}_{m}(q)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{lq})}{=}h(z,\bar{z})^{-m}\int_{(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}d\hat{v}_{m}(q)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{3.29-5})+(\ref{3-19.5})}{=}h(z,\bar{z})^{-m}\frac{2\varepsilon_{j}}{2\pi}.

In view of (
7.56
), (
7.55
) and

)
and the relation of the measures as given in (

).

To proceed further, let us set up some more notation. In (

) the

\displaystyle(\Psi_{\pm,m}\circ K_{t}^{j,\pm}\circ\Psi_{\pm,m}^{-1})\circ\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm,m}\circ(K_{t}^{j,\pm}\circ\gamma_{\xi^{-1}}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}})\circ\Psi_{\pm,m}^{-1}

where we have written (see Notation

for

\gamma_{\xi^{-1}}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}:=\Psi_{\pm,m}^{-1}\circ\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}\circ\Psi_{\pm,m}:\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}|_{(z(x),0,1)}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}|_{(z(\xi^{-1}x),0,1)}

\in

\in

) for

.
For

\in

in Notation

\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}
hence

\in

is

Note that

\in

=

remains true. Writing

and using “

=

(7.61)

\displaystyle K_{t}^{j,\pm}(z(x),z(\xi^{-1}x))\circ\gamma_{\xi^{-1}}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}\circ K_{t}^{j,\pm}(z,\zeta)^{\dagger})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}\circ K_{t}^{j,\pm}(\zeta,z))^{\dagger}

where

and the associated

by our

. (

) will be

).

It is important to remark that for

(=

in the above

the (finite) isotropy group at

may not be the identity. This feature is crucial to our supertrace
evaluation later on.

Lemma 7.14
.

With the notation above, it holds that for
x\in D_{j},

|\xi|

=

1
and
\xi^{-1}x\in D_{j},

\gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}

is an isometry on the bundle part; see also Corollary
8.5
). It holds
that

(7.62)

\gamma_{\xi^{-1}}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}=(\gamma_{\xi}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}})^{\dagger}.

Proof.

Since the action may involve the large angle one, let us be specific about
the proof. Observe that

(7.63)

\displaystyle\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}(\psi_{j}^{\ast}(\pi^{\ast}\eta^{I_{q}}\otimes e^{E})w^{m})_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{j}^{\ast}(\pi^{\ast}\eta^{I_{q}})_{\xi^{-1}x}\otimes\psi_{j}^{\ast}(\sigma^{E}(\xi^{-1})\circ e_{x}^{E})_{\xi^{-1}x}\xi^{m}w^{m}(\xi^{-1}x)

by the
\sigma
-invariance of the sections
\pi^{\ast}\eta^{I_{q}}.
Note that
(\sigma^{E}(\xi^{-1})\circ e_{x}^{E})_{\xi^{-1}x}

may not be
e_{\xi^{-1}x}^{E}
unless
\sigma(\xi)
is a small-angle
action. However it remains true that

(7.64)

||(\sigma^{E}(\xi^{-1})\circ e_{x}^{E})_{\xi^{-1}x}||_{h_{E}}=||e_{\xi^{-1}x}^{E}||_{h_{E}}

by the
\sigma^{E}
-invariance of the metric

and the
choice of local invariant section

Also by the
\sigma
-invariance
of the metrics
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}
for
\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{M}
and (
7.64
), we conclude from (
7.63
) that

(7.65)

||\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}(\psi_{j}^{\ast}(\pi^{\ast}\eta^{I_{q}}\otimes e^{E})w^{m})_{x}||_{\Lambda_{m}^{0,\ast}}=||(\psi_{j}^{\ast}(\pi^{\ast}\eta^{I_{q}}\otimes e^{E})w^{m})_{\xi^{-1}x}||_{\Lambda_{m}^{0,\ast}}\text{ }|\xi|^{m}.

Hence for
|\xi|

=

1
we learn that
\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}
is an isometry with respect to the metric
||

\cdot

||_{\Lambda_{m}^{0,\ast}}.
Together with (

)/(
7.54
) it
follows that for
|\xi|

=

1

\gamma_{\xi^{-1}}^{\mathcal{E}^{m\pm}}=\Psi_{\pm,m}^{-1}\circ\sigma(\xi)_{\xi^{-1}x}^{\ast}\circ\Psi_{\pm,m}
is an isometry with respect to the metric

\cdot

||_{\mathcal{E}^{m}}
(hence

). So taking

\in

\in

we have

which gives (
7.62
).

8.
Local
m
-index formula

In this long section we want to get an explicit expression of the local
index density, to which the first three subsections are devoted. The
remaining two subsections compare our index formula with the one – which we
view as a pure orbifold result, of Duistermaat.

Before proceeding to Subsection
8.1
let us discuss a number of
auxiliary results, which may be regarded as background material for the
subsequent subsections. First, we have the following “supertrace” integral equality by the McKean-Singer formula (Theorem
5.12
) together with (
6.81
) of Theorem
6.18
using
the approximate heat kernel
P_{m,t}^{0,\pm}
(noting that the integral of
l(x)^{m}dv_{\Sigma,m}
over
\Sigma
is finite as in Remark
3.4
):

Theorem 8.1
.

For
m\geq 0
,
a

>

\frac{m}{2}
(on which the metric
G_{a,m}
depends)
,
we have

(8.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}[Tre^{-t\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c+}}(x,x)-Tre^{-t\tilde{\square}_{m}^{c-}}(x,x)]dv_{\Sigma,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Sigma}[TrP_{m,t}^{0,+}(x,x)-TrP_{m,t}^{0,-}(x,x)]dv_{\Sigma,m}.

Thus, we are reduced to computing the supertrace of
P_{m,t}^{0}(x,x),

which is done in the first two subsections (cf. (
8.29
) and (
8.56
) below). We prove Theorem
1.1
in the third
subsection.

Our actual computation starts with (
8.20
). But before doing it we need
some preparatory work. From Section 2 we learn that the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action
\sigma
on
\Sigma
gives rise to a complex orbifold structure on

\Sigma/\sigma
(see Theorem
2.3
). Write
W_{j}

=

V_{j}\times(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
for a chart of
\Sigma
(see Notation
6.1
) where
V_{j}
is chosen to be a complex
orbifold chart of
\Sigma/\sigma
(see the proof of Theorem
2.3
).
Moreover the finite group associated to the orbifold chart
V_{j}
is a
cyclic subgroup of
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, denoted by
G_{j}
;
W_{j}
is suitably shrinked so that
G_{j}
is the largest isotropy group at
some point
x

\in

W_{j}
, containing isotropy groups at any
y

\in

W_{j}
as subgroups, cf. Corollary
8.6
. Let
g_{0}\in G_{j}
be a
generator of order
N_{j}+1

(8.2)

g_{0}^{N_{j}+1}=1\text{ and }g_{k}:=g_{0}^{k},\text{ \ \ }k\geq 1\text{ \ (note }g_{1}=g_{0}\text{).}

Each
g_{k}
depends on
j
but for the simplicity of notation we
omit “
j
” in the expression of the symbol
g_{k}.
Define
(possibly after shrinking
V_{j})

(8.3)

\gamma_{k}^{-1}(\in\sigma(S^{1})):=\pi_{V_{j}}\circ\sigma(g_{k}^{-1}):V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}\rightarrow V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}

for all
k

=

1,

\cdot\cdot,

N_{j},
where
\pi_{V_{j}}

is the natural projection from
W_{j}
onto
V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
;
note that

(8.4)

\gamma_{k}\text{ is denoted by }\tau(g_{k})\in\sigma(S^{1})\text{ in the
proof of Theorem \ref{thm2-1}}.

Lemma 8.2
.

With notations in the proof of Lemma
7.3
suppose
x,

x^{\prime}

\in

W_{j}
with
w(x)=w(x^{\prime}).
Assume that
e^{i\beta}\circ x
and
e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime}
lie in
W_{j}
for
some
e^{i\beta}\in S^{1}.
Then
w(e^{i\beta}\circ x)

=

w(e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime}).

Proof.

First assume that
x^{\prime}
is close to
x.
As in the proof of Lemma
7.3
, we take a sequence of points
x_{l}

\in

D_{l}\cap D_{l-1}
(resp.
x_{l}^{\prime}

\in

D_{l}\cap D_{l-1}),

l=0,

1,

\cdot\cdot,

L
such that
x_{0}=x,

\cdot\cdot,

x_{L+1}

=

e^{i\beta}\circ x

\in

D_{0}\cap D_{L}
(resp.
x_{0}^{\prime}=x^{\prime},

\cdot\cdot,

x_{L+1}^{\prime}

=

e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime}

\in

D_{0}\cap D_{L}).
Let (
z_{l},w_{l})
denote the
coordinates of the patch
D_{l}
with
D_{0}

=

D_{L+1}

=

W_{j}.

Note that
w_{0}

=

w_{L+1}

=

w.
By (
2.3
) in Proposition
2.1
we have

(8.5)

w_{l}(x_{l})=w_{l-1}(x_{l})f_{l}(z_{l}(x_{l})),\text{ }l=1,\cdot\cdot,L+1

where
f_{l}
is holomorphic in
z_{l}.
From (
7.17
) and (
7.18
) (replacing
\gamma_{l}
by
\beta_{l}
) it follows that
w_{l}(x_{l+1})

=

w_{l}(e^{i\beta_{l}}x_{l})

=

e^{i\beta_{l}}w_{l}(x_{l})
. Together with (
8.5
) and
\beta
:
=

\sum_{l=0}^{L}\beta_{l}
we obtain

(8.6)

\displaystyle w(e^{i\beta}\circ x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w_{L+1}(x_{L+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i\beta}w(x)f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1})).

Similarly we have

(8.7)

w(e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime})=e^{i\beta}w(x^{\prime})f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}^{\prime})).

By Lemma
7.6

i)
we have
|
w(e^{i\beta}\circ x)|

=

|w(x)
|
(resp.
|
w(e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime})|

=

|w(x^{\prime})
|
) and hence

(8.8)

\displaystyle|f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle(\text{resp. }|f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}^{\prime}))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1)

in view of (
8.6
) (resp. (
8.7
)). The actions
e^{i\beta_{l}}
are holomorphic and hence, in terms of
x

\displaystyle f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}(z_{1}(e^{i\beta_{0}}\circ x))f_{2}(z_{2}(e^{i(\beta_{1}+\beta_{0})}\circ x))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(e^{i\beta}\circ x))

is holomorphic in
x.
This together with (
8.8
) implies
that
f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}))
is
independent of
x
so that it is the same as
f_{1}(z_{1}(x_{1}^{\prime}))\cdot\cdot\cdot f_{L+1}(z_{L+1}(x_{L+1}^{\prime}))
(here
x\sim x^{\prime}
so
\beta_{l},

\beta_{l}^{\prime}
can be chosen to be the
same)
.
In view of (
8.6
) and (
8.7
) we conclude that
w(e^{i\beta}\circ x)

=

w(e^{i\beta}\circ x^{\prime}).
For the
general case where
x
and
x^{\prime}
are not necessarily close, one
connects
x
and
x^{\prime}
by a path
\alpha(t)

\subset

W_{j}

with the same
w(\alpha(t))
values. The proof follows by the usual
continuity argument.

For the fixed point set

(
\subset V_{j}
) of

Remark 8.3
.

i)
We can choose the above chart
W_{j}
(
=

V_{j}\times

(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times

such that

from (

itself for all

).
ii)
Note that the slice

\subset

depends on the choice of local coordinates of
\Sigma
.

Define

:=

\{z^{\prime}

\in

|

=

(z^{\prime},0,1)\}
and

\subset

\Sigma,
the fixed point set of

so

=

\Sigma^{g_{k}}

\cap

V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
. Let
V_{j}^{F}
:
=

\cup_{k=1}^{N_{j}}V_{j}^{g_{k}}.
Note that for
1\leq k\leq N_{j}

(8.10)

V_{j}^{g_{k}}\neq V_{j}.

Otherwise
\sigma(g_{k})
will fix all points in
V_{j}
and
hence one sees that
W_{j}

\subset

\Sigma^{g_{k}}.
Since
W_{j}
is
open in
\Sigma,
it follows by holomorphicity that
g_{k}

=

\{1\},
a
contradiction to

.

We have from (
8.3
) and Lemma
7.6

i)
that

(8.11)

\gamma_{k}^{-1}x={\Large\{}\begin{array}[]{c}\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)})x\text{ \ \ \ \ }x\in(V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F})\times\{0\}\times\{1\}\\
x\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ }x\in V_{j}^{g_{k}}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}\text{ \ \ \ \ }\end{array}

where
\eta_{k}
is a real valued, continuous function (at least
locally defined at a given
x)
. Based on Lemma
8.2
we have

Proposition 8.4
.

With the chart
W_{j}
chosen in Remark
8.3
and the
notations above, we have
i)

e^{i\eta_{k}}
is a constant, independent of
the choice of
x\in(V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F})\times\{0\}\times\{1\};

ii)

\gamma_{k}^{-1}

=

\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})
acting on
V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
itself is an isometry with respect to the metric induced
from
G_{a,m}
;
iii)

e^{i\eta_{k}}=g_{k}^{-1}.
In particular
\gamma_{k}^{-1}
equals
\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})

=

\sigma(g_{k}^{-1})
on
V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
; namely
\pi_{V_{j}}
in the definition (
8.3
) of
\gamma_{k}^{-1}
can be dropped.

Proof.

We will simply write
e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x
for
\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)})x.

For
x,x^{\prime}\in(V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F})\times\{0\}\times\{1\},

w(x)=w(x^{\prime})=1
which by Lemma
8.2
gives that
w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x)

=

w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x^{\prime})
(for
x^{\prime}
close
to
x
so that
e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x^{\prime}
falls in
W_{j}
and is close
to
e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x).
By the definition of
\eta_{k}

w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x)=1=w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x^{\prime})}x^{\prime})
and we get
w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x^{\prime})}x^{\prime})

=

w(e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}x^{\prime}).
By Lemma
8.2
again, applying
e^{-i\eta_{k}(x)}
to the
arguments of
w
we conclude
w(e^{i(\eta_{k}(x^{\prime})-\eta_{k}(x))}x^{\prime})=w(x^{\prime})
which is
1.
In view of the action by
a small angle (by the continuity of
\eta_{k}
and
x^{\prime}\sim x
) it
follows that for
x^{\prime}
near
x
,
e^{i(\eta_{k}(x^{\prime})-\eta_{k}(x))}x^{\prime}

=

x^{\prime}
so that
e^{i\eta_{k}(x^{\prime})}

=

e^{i\eta_{k}(x)}
since
x^{\prime}

\notin

V_{j}^{F}
. Therefore

e^{i\eta_{k}}
is constant in each connected component of
(V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F})\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
by continuity. Now
V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F}
is connected since
V_{j}^{g_{k}}
is of real
codimension
\geq 2
in
V_{j}
by the holomorphicity of
g_{k}
. We have
shown
i)
.

From
i)
and (
8.11
) we have now that
\gamma_{k}^{-1}

=

\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})
on
V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
(including
V_{j}^{g_{k}}\times\{0\}\times\{1\})
by continuity from
V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F}
to its closure
V_{j}.
That
\gamma_{k}^{-1}
is an isometry
follows from the fact that the
\sigma(S^{1})
-action is an isometry (see
Remark
7.4
). We have shown
ii)
.

To show
iii),
we fix an
x_{0}=(z^{\prime},0,1)
with
z^{\prime}

\in

V_{j}^{g_{k}}.
Then
\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})x_{0}=\gamma_{k}^{-1}x_{0}

by
ii)
and
\gamma_{k}^{-1}x_{0}

=
(
\pi_{V_{j}}\circ\sigma(g_{k}^{-1}))x_{0}
=
\sigma(g_{k}^{-1})x_{0}

=

x_{0}
since
\pi_{V_{j}}
is trivially the identity on
V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
from
its definition
.
It follows that we can put

(8.12)

e^{i\eta_{k}}=h_{x_{0}}g_{k}^{-1}

for some
h_{x_{0}}

\in

G_{j}

\subset

S^{1}.
Next, we
apply the action

\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})\overset{ii)}{=}\gamma_{k}^{-1}\overset{(\ref{Str-5})}{=}\pi_{V_{j}}\circ\sigma(g_{k}^{-1})

to any
x
near
x_{0}.
We have
\sigma(e^{i\eta_{k}})(x)=\sigma(e^{i\epsilon_{k}}g_{k}^{-1})(x)
for a small angle
\epsilon_{k}
(depending on
x

a priori
) obtained by
\pi_{V_{j}};
compare (
2.12
) and the lines below it. From the global
freeness of the
\sigma(S^{1})
-action at
x

\in

(V_{j}\backslash V_{j}^{F})\times\{0\}\times\{1\}
it follows that
e^{i\eta_{k}}

=

e^{i\epsilon_{k}}g_{k}^{-1}
for such
x.
Comparing this with (
8.12
)
gives

(8.13)

h_{x_{0}}=e^{i\epsilon_{k}}.

The contradiction is arising: The number of
h_{x_{0}}
is at most

|G_{j}|,
while by choosing
x
sufficiently near
x_{0}
and applying the
continuity argument at
x_{0}
as just mentioned one can make the angle
\epsilon_{k}(x)
arbitrarily small. This would violate (
8.13
)
unless
\epsilon_{k}

=

0
thus
h_{x_{0}}

=

1,
giving
e^{i\eta_{k}}

=

g_{k}^{-1}
by (
8.12
). The claim
iii)
of the proposition
is now proved.

The following two corollaries are needed for later use.

Corollary 8.5
.

\gamma_{k}^{-1}:V_{j}\rightarrow V_{j}
is also an isometry
with respect to the metric
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}|_{V_{j}}.

Proof.

Let
i:V_{j}\rightarrow\Sigma
be the natural embedding. By Proposition
8.4
one has
\gamma_{k}

=

\sigma(g_{k})|_{V_{j}},
giving that

\gamma_{k}^{\ast}i^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}g_{M}

=

i^{\ast}\sigma(g_{k})^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}g_{M}

=

i^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}g_{M}
using
\pi\circ\sigma(g_{k})

=

\pi.

Corollary 8.6
.

The map
\tau:G_{j}\rightarrow\tau(G_{j})
used in Theorem
2.3
is a group isomorphism.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.4

iii)
and (
8.4
) we learn that
\tau(g_{k})

=

\sigma(g_{k}).
If
\tau(g_{k})
is the identity on
V_{j},

this contradicts (
8.10
)
.
So
\ker\tau

=

\{1\}
hence
\tau

is a group isomorphism since in Theorem
2.3
we have shown that
\tau
is a group homomorphism.

Recall (in the proof of Lemma

) that
\alpha_{k}(x)

\in

\subset

is chosen to satisfy the property
that for

\alpha_{k}(x)x
(

\in W_{j}
and

=

For

set

(8.14)

\alpha_{0}(x)=e^{-i\phi(x)}\text{ for }x=(z,\phi,r).

Clearly

=

0.
We describe the

\alpha_{k}(x)
below.

Proposition 8.7
.

(group property of
\alpha_{k}
in the proof of Lemma
6.14
) With the chart
W_{j}
chosen as in Remark
8.3
and the notation
above, it holds that

is independent of

and

(8.15)

\{\alpha_{k}(x)\alpha_{0}^{-1}(x):k=0,1,\cdot\cdot,\Lambda\}=G_{j}

(see (

) for

and (

) for

). So

Moreover we can arrange
1

\leq

\leq

N_{j}

such that

(8.16)

\alpha_{k}(x)\alpha_{0}^{-1}(x)=g_{k}^{-1}.

In particular, the set
\{\alpha_{k}(x)\}_{k=0,1,\cdot\cdot\cdot,\Lambda}
is independent of those

with
\phi(x)=0
and this set forms
a group equal to the local orbifold group
G_{j}.

Proof.

Let
x_{1}=(z,0,1)

\in

V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}.
It follows from
the definition of
\alpha_{k}(x_{1})
and Lemma
7.6

i)
that
\sigma(\alpha_{k}(x_{1}))x_{1}

=

(z_{k},0,1)

\in

V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\}.
Since
V_{j}
is an orbifold chart, there is

\in

G_{j}
such that
\tau(g_{l})z=z_{k}
(see the proof of Theorem
2.3
). By Proposition
8.4

iii)
(noting

cf. (
8.4
))
\sigma(g_{l})x_{1}

=

\sigma(\alpha_{k}(x_{1}))x_{1}.
So

lies in the isotropy
group of
x_{1},
which is a subgroup of
G_{j}.
It follows that
\alpha_{k}(x_{1})\in G_{j}.
Denote the set
\{\alpha_{k}(x)\alpha_{0}^{-1}(x):k=0,1,\cdot\cdot,\Lambda\}
by
\Gamma_{x}.
Observing
\alpha_{0}(x_{1})

=

1
by (

), we have shown

(8.17)

\Gamma_{x_{1}}\subset G_{j}.

Conversely by Proposition
8.4

iii)
again any element of
G_{j}
acts on

itself via
\sigma,
so from

\alpha_{k}(x_{1}),
it follows that

Together with (
8.17
) we conclude

(8.18)

\Gamma_{x_{1}}=G_{j}.

For
x=(z,\phi,r)

\in

W_{j}
we compute (
\sigma
omitted
for simplicity of notation)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(x)\alpha_{k}(x_{1})x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\phi(x)}\alpha_{k}(x_{1})(re^{i\phi(x)}x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\alpha_{k}(x_{1})x_{1}=(z_{k},0,r),

so
\phi(\alpha_{0}(x)\alpha_{k}(x_{1})x)=0.
It follows that

\subset

\{\alpha_{k}(x)\}_{k}
from the definition of
\alpha_{k}(x),
so up to

on both sets
\{\alpha_{k}(x_{1})\}_{k}

=

\Gamma_{x_{1}}

(\alpha_{0}(x_{1})

=

1)

\subset

\Gamma_{x}.

Similarly one proves

=

0,

giving
\Gamma_{x}

\subset\Gamma_{x_{1}}
so
\Gamma_{x_{1}}

=

\Gamma_{x}.
This, together with (
8.18
), gives (
8.15
). By the
continuity of
\alpha_{k}(x)\alpha_{0}^{-1}(x)
in
x
and the

\alpha_{k}(x)\alpha_{0}^{-1}(x)
for each

1.1
as stated in the Introduction. To fix the notation let

be

;

Recall that the metric
G_{a}
or

on
\Sigma
has the property that “base”

-slices and “fibre”
w
-slices are orthogonally

) for a precise discussion). We summarize the
results as follows.

Lemma 8.10
.

With notations above and those in the Introduction, we have
i)
the quotient (Hermitian) metric
g_{quot}
on
T^{1,0}(\Sigma)/L_{\Sigma}
defined by the restriction of
G_{a,m}
on the orthogonal
complement of the tangent space to the “fibre” (the orbit
of the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action) is isometric to
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}
and
is
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant;
ii)
for
E
being
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-equivariant with a
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant Hermitian metric
h_{E},

Td(E,h_{E})
and
ch(E,h_{E})
are
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant, denoted
by
Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})
and
ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})

respectively.

Recall that
||\cdot||
denotes the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant
Hermitian metric on
L_{\Sigma}
(Step 1 in Section
3
)
,

which induces
||\cdot||^{\ast}
and
||\cdot||_{m}^{\ast}
on
L_{\Sigma}^{\ast}
and
(L_{\Sigma}^{\ast})^{m}
respectively. As above
we write the first Chern form
c_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||).

We are going to establish “Part I” of our transversal local
index density in Theorem
8.11
via the local adaptation of classical
local index density arguments (non-transversal ones) with (
8.27
).

Theorem 8.11
.

With notations and assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
, we have

(8.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\text{Part I of }StrP_{m,t}^{0}(x,x)dv_{\Sigma,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\delta_{p|m}[Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(T^{1,0}\Sigma/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})\wedge ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})\wedge e^{-mc_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||)}\wedge d\hat{v}_{m}]_{2n}(x)

(in a pointwise, non-uniform manner) for
x

\in

\Sigma_{p}
(=
\Sigma\backslash\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
)
.
Moreover

(8.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\int_{\Sigma}\text{Part I of }StrP_{m,t}^{0}(x,x)\text{ }dv_{\Sigma,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\delta_{p|m}\int_{\Sigma}Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(T^{1,0}\Sigma/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})\wedge ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})\wedge e^{-mc_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||)}\wedge d\hat{v}_{m}.

Proof.

Let
N_{0}(n)
be as in
ii)
of Theorem
1.3
. We first claim that for
every positive integer
N_{0}

\geq

N_{0}(n)
(
=n+1)
, there exist
\delta

>

0
and
C_{N_{0}}

>

0
such that

(8.30)

\displaystyle|\text{Part I of (}TrP_{m,t}^{0,+}(x,x)-TrP_{m,t}^{0,-}(x,x))

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ \ \ }-p\delta_{p|m}l^{m}(x)\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}t^{-(n-1)+j}(Trb_{n-1-j}^{+}(z)-Trb_{n-1-j}^{-}(z))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N_{0}}l^{m}(x)t^{-(n-1)+N_{0}+1}

for any
t,

0<t<\delta
and any
x

\in

\Sigma_{p}
(=
\Sigma\backslash\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
)
.
For
p=1
the proof of (
8.30
) follows from (
7.52
) (with
\zeta

=

z)
and (
8.27
).
Since
l^{m}(x)
in (
8.30
) blows up as
|w(x)|

\rightarrow

\infty,
no uniform convergence follows from (
8.30
). In fact the
convergence cannot be uniform; this follows from an inspection of the factor

l^{m}(x)
in (
7.52
) and (
8.27
) used for deriving (
8.30
).
For
p

>

1
there is an extra factor
p\delta_{p|m}
as shown before
(see (
7.51
) and the paragraph after it). Hence the claim. It is
known by using rescaling techniques that

(8.31)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}t^{-(n-1)+j}(Trb_{n-1-j}^{+}(z)-Trb_{n-1-j}^{-}(z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=n-1}^{N_{0}}t^{-(n-1)+j}(Trb_{n-1-j}^{+}(z)-Trb_{n-1-j}^{-}(z))

as is from
[
6
, Proposition 3.21 and Theorem 4.1 (1)]
(about
the vanishing of the supertrace for degrees strictly less than
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M

=

2(n-1)
). Note that only the
t^{0}
-term
Trb_{0}^{+}(z)-Trb_{0}^{-}(z)
(
j=n-1
) in (
8.31
) would survive as
t\rightarrow 0
.
We obtain via (
8.30
) and (
8.31
)

(8.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\text{Part I of }StrP_{m,t}^{0}(x,x)\text{ }dv_{\Sigma,m}

\displaystyle\overset{}{=}\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}(x)p\delta_{p|m}l^{m}(x)(Trb_{0}^{+}(z)-Trb_{0}^{-}(z))dv_{V_{i}}(z)dv_{m}(|w|)dv(\phi)/2\pi.

The RHS of (
8.32
) is seen to be related to the classical local index
density. For, in view of the heat kernel
K_{t}^{i}(z,\zeta)
for
\square_{V_{i},m}^{c}
(see (

) and (

)) the well-known local
index density computation (in connection with Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch
theorem, cf.
[

]
) can be adapted and applied on

(with the
extra bundle

), and then, since the background metric
g_{M}
on
V_{i}

for
\square_{V_{i},m}^{c}
(cf. Step 3 of Section
3
and
Definition
3.8
) has been identified with the above-mentioned metric
g_{quot}
on

(Lemma
8.10
)
,
we arrive
at the following equalities:

(8.33)

\displaystyle(Trb_{0}^{+}(z)-Trb_{0}^{-}(z))dv_{V_{i}}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[Td(T^{1,0}V_{i},\pi^{\ast}g_{M})ch(\psi_{i}^{\ast}(E\otimes(L_{\Sigma}^{\ast})^{m})|_{V_{i}},h_{E}\otimes||\cdot||_{m}^{\ast})]_{2(n-1)}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[Td(T^{1,0}(\Sigma)/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})ch(E,h_{E})ch((L_{\Sigma}^{\ast})^{m},||\cdot||_{m}^{\ast})]_{2(n-1)}(z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(T^{1,0}(\Sigma)/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})e^{-mc_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||)}]_{2(n-1)}(z,w)

where

:

(z,w)

\in

\rightarrow\Sigma
is the local trivialization (see (

)
and (

)) and

may be identified with

\subset

\Sigma.
By (

) we write (

) as

(8.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}(x)p\delta_{p|m}l^{m}(x)(Trb_{0}^{+}(z)-Trb_{0}^{-}(z))dv_{V_{i}}(z)dv_{f,m}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{7-38.75})}{=}

\displaystyle p\delta_{p|m}[Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(T^{1,0}(\Sigma)/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ e^{-mc_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||)}]_{2(n-1)}(z,w)\wedge l^{m}(x)dv_{f,m}\text{ (also by }\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}(x)=1)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{fibrenv_0})}{=}

\displaystyle p\delta_{p|m}[Td_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(T^{1,0}\Sigma/L_{\Sigma},g_{quot})\wedge ch_{\mathbb{C}^{\ast}}(E,h_{E})\wedge e^{-mc_{1}(L_{\Sigma},||\cdot||)}\wedge d\hat{v}_{m}]_{2n}(x).

The claim (

) follows from (

) and (

). To exchange the limit

and the integral sign we
note that

in the RHS of (

) is an

function in

, implying the second claim (

).

8.2.
Part II of the local
m
-index formula (
k\geq 1
in (
8.23
)) via Lefschetz type formulas.

In contrast to the
k=0
case
,

\gamma_{k}^{\mathcal{E}^{m}}
is not
necessarily the identity endomorphism if
k\geq 1
in (
8.23
). In
view of local equivariant index theorems, the fixed points set
V_{j}^{\gamma_{k}}

(
in
V_{j},
identified with
(V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\})^{\gamma_{k}}

\subset

V_{j}\times\{0\}\times\{1\})

of
\gamma_{k}
and hence the singular stratum
\Sigma_{j,k}
(see (
8.9
)) (which may cover
|\Sigma_{j,k}|
–the support of
\Sigma_{j,k},

several times) are expected to play a role in the final index formula. See
the remark below for the support
|\Sigma_{j,k}|
of
\Sigma_{j,k}
.

Remark 8.12
.

Let
O

:=

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\circ S
be the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-orbit of a set
S
. We write
|O|
for the “support of
O
”, that is, the set-theoretical image of the
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-action on
S
. This counts the points in the orbit only once.

Notation 8.13
.

In the remaining of this section
\sigma_{\bullet}
denotes
the symbol map as in
[
6
, Definition 3.4 and Proposition 3.6]
and for
\sigma_{\bullet}(endomorphisms
) see
[
6
, Lemma 6.10 and the top two
lines on p.193]
which is basically the symbol of the Clifford algebra
part of
endomorphisms
.

To proceed with (

for

We use

. The following basically follows from definitions.

Lemma 8.14
.

With the notation above, it holds that (
\mathcal{G}
being a
finite set)

(8.45)

\Sigma_{\text{sing}}=\mathop{\displaystyle\bigcup}\limits_{\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{G},\text{ }\tilde{g}\neq 1}\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}.

Proof.

Recall that
\Sigma_{p_{j}}
denotes the set of points having period
2\pi/p_{j}
and that
\Sigma_{\text{sing}}
is by definition the union of
\Sigma_{p_{j}},

j\geq 2.
If a point has the period less than
2\pi/p_{1},
then its isotropy group
\subset

S^{1}
is nontrivial so that it
is a fixed point of some element
\tilde{g}
of
S^{1}.
Conversely, any
point in
\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}
for
\tilde{g}

\neq

1
has a nontrivial
isotropy group rendering its period less than
2\pi/p_{1}.

Notation 8.15
.

i)

Here we think of

Lemma 8.16
.

With the notation above
\hat{A}_{BGV}(T\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}/L_{\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}},g_{quot})
,
R^{1}
and
\tilde{g}_{1}
are locally

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant (meaning that it is invariant under the

with

\in

\mathbb{R}^{+}
and

small). Moreover
\det^{1/2}(1-\tilde{g}_{1})
and

in (

) are also locally
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant.

Proof.

By (

) and (

) we write the metric

in special

for (

) at

\in

and

=

\in

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
for

\in

\mathbb{R}^{+}
arbitrary,

sufficiently near

such that
\sigma(c)q_{0}
still lies in
\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}\cap D_{j}
,
as follows (with the same coordinates (

) holds at

when

(8.50)

\displaystyle G_{a,m}|_{\sigma(c)q_{0}}=(g_{M})_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}(z_{0},\bar{z}_{0})dz_{\alpha}d\bar{z}_{\beta}+

\displaystyle{\Large(}\varphi_{1}(|c|^{2}w_{0}\bar{w}_{0})+\varphi_{2}(|c|^{2}w_{0}\bar{w}_{0})4a^{2}|c|^{-4a-2}(w_{0}\bar{w}_{0})^{-2a-1}{\large)}\frac{dwd\bar{w}}{\lambda_{m}}.

It is not difficult to see from (

) that the normal space

which is perpendicular to

at

respect to
\pi^{\ast}g_{M}

=

(g_{M})_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}(z_{0},\bar{z}_{0})

dz_{\alpha}d\bar{z}_{\beta}
in

=

\psi_{j}^{-1}(D_{j})
, and hence the curvature

of

is

\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
-invariant. For

) so that

follows from that the action of

just mentioned.

Remark 8.17
.

By (

) the metric

and the metric

coincide on the normal bundle

.

Corollary 8.18
.

With the notation above,
\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{g},m}|_{\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}\cap D_{j}}
(=

\in
(

z

\in

-invariant.

Proof.

From Lemma

I(\tilde{g})

in

.

Using Lemma

we prove

Lemma 8.19
.

For

\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}.

.

Corollary 8.20
.

For all

\in

Proof.

-invariant by Corollary

).

Note that

\subset

\subset

Proof.

Proposition 8.23
.

With the notation above, we have

(8.54)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{-m}\int_{\Sigma^{\tilde{g}}}\overline{\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{g},m}(x)}l^{m}(x)dv_{\Sigma^{\tilde{g}},m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(j,k):g_{k\text{ }}^{(j)}=\tilde{g}}(g_{k}^{-1})^{m}\int_{V_{j}^{\gamma_{k}}\times(-\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{j})\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\varphi_{j}(z,\phi)\overline{\mathcal{F}_{k,m}^{j}(z,\bar{z})}

\displaystyle\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ }d\tilde{v}_{V_{j}^{\gamma_{k}}}(z)\wedge d\hat{v}_{m}(z,\phi,|w|).

Recall that the largest period is
\frac{2\pi}{p}
(see the paragraph after
